                                         Abstract
                                         A critical bottleneck hindering further advancement in embodied AI and robotics is the challenge of scaling robot data.
                                         To address this, the field of learning robot manipulation skills from human video data has attracted rapidly growing
                                         attention in recent years, driven by the abundance of human activity videos and advances in computer vision. This
                                         line of research promises to enable robots to acquire skills passively from the vast and readily available resource of




arXiv:2604.27621v1 [cs.RO] 30 Apr 2026
                                         human demonstrations, substantially favoring scalable learning for generalist robotic systems. Therefore, we present
                                         this survey to provide a comprehensive and up-to-date review of human-video-based learning techniques in robotics,
                                         focusing on both human-robot skill transfer and data foundations. We first review the policy learning foundations in
                                         robotics, and then describe the fundamental interfaces to incorporate human videos. Subsequently, we introduce a
                                         hierarchical taxonomy of transferring human videos to robot skills, covering task-, observation-, and action-oriented
                                         pathways, along with a cross-family analysis of their couplings with different data configurations and learning paradigms.
                                         In addition, we investigate the data foundations including widely-used human video datasets and video generation
                                         schemes, and provide large-scale statistical trends in dataset development and utilization. Ultimately, we emphasize the
                                         challenges and limitations intrinsic to this field, and delineate potential avenues for future research. The paper list of our
                                         survey is available at https://github.com/IRMVLab/awesome-robot-learning-from-human-videos.


                                         Keywords
                                         Human-Robot Skill Transfer, Video-Based Learning, Robot Manipulation, Imitation Learning, Reinforcement Learning

as a bridge, task intents as a bridge do not require                                                               intuitively represent task objectives across different phases,

Table 8. Statistics of dependence on real-world robot data in LfHV studies.


Kuang et al. 2024; Papagiannis et al. 2025; Hsu et al. 2025;
Zhang et al. 2025a).
   As can be noted in recent studies, the LfHV community
has not fully eliminated the need for real-world robot
data. Although a meaningful subset of works support
real-world deployment using only human videos, their
robustness in complex target scenarios, especially under out-
of-distribution conditions, still requires further improvement.
In most existing works, real-robot demonstrations or
interactions are still needed to generate robot-specific
policies with feasible kinematics and closed-loop correction.
Thus, the present evidence suggests that human videos can
substantially serve as one layer of the embodied data pyramid
(see Fig. 18), but cannot yet completely replace real robot
data in general for the development of generalist agents.
                                                                                  Figure 18. Data pyramids defined by Bjorck et al. (2025); Bi
                                                                                  et al. (2025a); Wen et al. (2025), respectively.

but how to support sustained contributions from individual      Robot learning from human videos has emerged as a
researchers, academic labs, and industry partners under a       promising route toward alleviating the data bottleneck in

beyond one-off static releases. Instead, it should support      and data foundations. We first revisited how human videos

unified storage and access interfaces, and manipulation-        existing methods into a hierarchical taxonomy spanning task-
relevant annotations that are directly useful for downstream    , observation-, and action-oriented transfer. Under this view,
robot learning. Such an ecosystem may integrate egocentric      human videos can support robot learning through multiple
human videos with 3D hand and head poses, language              forms of information flow, including task structures, task
descriptions, and richer multimodal data like aforementioned    intents, transformed videos, visual embeddings, affordances,
audio, gaze, and tactile signals. It should also lower          and latent actions. Beyond taxonomy construction, we
the barrier to contribution through lightweight collection      further discussed how these transfer families differ in their
and crowdsourced annotation schemes, while maintaining          couplings with viewpoint choices, dependence on robot data,
reproducibility through standardized processing and shared      and learning paradigms. This highlights the characteristic
evaluation protocols across labs and robot embodiments.         tradeoffs underlying current LfHV designs. Based on the
Building this kind of ecosystem would substantially reduce      taxonomy and cross-family comparisons, we also provide
duplicated efforts and provide a stronger foundation for        practical guidelines for selecting suitable routes to develop
scalable, multimodal, and reproducible LfHV research.           LfHV models.
